People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
I guess what brings me in is… I’ve just stopped wanting to do the things that usually keep me going. Like salsa nights—I used to love that, even if my knees complain—and church gatherings, cooking for the neighbors, little things like that. 

Even walking into my shop, you know, just to smell the wood and be around the tools… I used to enjoy that. Now I kind of just don’t want to. It’s strange. I mean, I’ve been a carpenter a long time, and cedar still smells like cedar, but it doesn’t pull me in the way it used to.

From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n2\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF32.0, F32.1, F32.2, F32.3, F32.4, F32.5, F32.8, F32.9\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. Like salsa nights—I used to love that, even if my knees complain—and church gatherings, cooking for the neighbors, little things like that. \n\nEven walking into my shop, you know, just to smell the wood and be around the tools… I used to enjoy that. Now I kind of just don’t want to. It’s strange. I mean, I’ve been a carpenter a long time, and cedar still smells like cedar, but it doesn’t pull me in the way it used to.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been feeling this way, not wanting to do the things you used to enjoy?\n\nPatient: It’s been… about six months, I think. Long enough that I stopped telling myself it was just a rough week, you know? ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Honestly… not much. That’s the problem, I guess. The things I used to look forward to—salsa nights, church gatherings, cooking something for the neighbors—those used to kind of keep me moving. Now I just don’t have the pull toward them.

Even the shop… I used to walk in there and just the smell of the wood, the tools laid out, that would make me feel like myself. Cedar, oak, all that. I still notice it, but it doesn’t really give me that same feeling anymore. I keep thinking, “Maybe tomorrow I’ll want to,” but mostly I just don’t.

Yeah… most mornings, really. I wake up and there’s this heavy, empty feeling right here in my chest. It’s like—this sounds odd—but like the color’s gone out of the house. My wife’ll be moving around, making coffee or whatever, and I’m trying to act normal, you know, not fall apart in front of her.

Empty. So yes, sad, empty… most of the time, especially when I wake up.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any significant change in your weight recently?\n\nPatient: I… I’m not really sure, to be honest. Now I just don’t have the pull toward them.\n\nEven the shop… I used to walk in there and just the smell of the wood, the tools laid out, that would make me feel like myself. Cedar, oak, all that. I still notice it, but it doesn’t really give me that same feeling anymore. I keep thinking, “Maybe tomorrow I’ll want to,” but mostly I just don’t.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling sad or empty most of the time?\n\nPatient: Yeah… most mornings, really. My wife’ll be moving around, making coffee or whatever, and I’m trying to act normal, you know, not fall apart in front of her.\n\nIt’s not just “having a bad morning.” It sits there. And then I start thinking about little things that need doing—there’s a loose hinge on one of our cabinets I still haven’t fixed, which is ridiculous for a carpenter—but even that feels… I don’t know. Empty. So yes, sad, empty… most of the time, especially when I wake up.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any significant change in your weight recently?\n\nPatient: I… I’m not really sure, to be honest. I haven’t been checking the scale much. But lately… I don’t know. I couldn’t tell you a number. Nothing I’ve clearly noticed as a big weight change.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling guilty or worthless about yourself or your situation?\n\nPatient: Yeah… guilty, definitely. It makes me feel pretty worthless sometimes.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. Yeah… people at the job have noticed it, actually. Some days I’m moving like my boots are full of wet cement. I’ll go to pick up a board—just a board, something I’ve done ten thousand times—and it takes me forever, like my body didn’t get the message.

There was this old foreman I worked with years back who used to say, “Don’t wear out the floor unless you’re carrying lumber,” and I keep thinking of that because I’m not actually getting anything done. Just… moving around, but stuck.

I… yeah. I don’t like saying that out loud.

It’s not like I’ve— I mean, I get these thoughts sometimes, like maybe it would be easier if I just didn’t wake up. Like if everything could just stop without me having to… do anything. irrelevant external stimuli.\n  manifestation: When I was in that keyed-up state, every saw noise, phone buzz, or bit of movement in the shop pulled me off track, and I would jump from one half-done call.\n- key: functional_impairment\n  name: Functional Impairment\n  manifestation: I have had to hand off parts of jobs I normally supervise myself, and I have pulled away from church, friends, and neighbors to the point that my wife and parish contact got worried about my safety.\n- key: duration\n  name: Duration\n  description: 2 months\n  manifestation: This whole change in my mood and I’ll just pace around the shop, rubbing my hands, circling the same bench like I’m looking for something. There was this old foreman I worked with years back who used to say, “Don’t wear out the floor unless you’re carrying lumber,” and I keep thinking of that because I’m not actually getting anything done. Just… moving around, but stuck.\n\nDoctor: Have you had any thoughts of hurting yourself or ending your life?\n\nPatient: I… yeah. It scares me when that comes up.\n\nI try to push it away, you know. Yeah… when I’m not actually doing something, I can get kind of stuck pacing. Around the shop mostly. I’ll rub my hands, go from one bench to another like I’m looking for something, but I’m not really doing anything useful.

Yeah, even sitting still, I guess it shows up. My hands don’t seem to know what to do with themselves sometimes—rubbing them together, shifting around.

What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: I guess what brings me in is… I’ve just stopped wanting to do the things that usually keep me going. Like salsa nights—I used to love that, even if my knees complain—and church gatherings, cooking for the neighbors, little things like that. \n\nEven walking into my shop, you know, just to smell the wood and be around the tools… I used to enjoy that. Now I kind of just don’t want to. It’s strange. I mean, I’ve been a carpenter a long time, and cedar still smells like cedar, but it doesn’t pull me in the way it used to.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been feeling this way, not wanting to do the things you used to enjoy?\n\nPatient: It’s been… about six months, I think. Long enough that I stopped telling myself it was just a rough week, you know? At first I figured maybe I was tired, or work was getting to me—carpentry can wear on you, especially at my age, and I had this one set of cabinets that just wouldn’t line up right—but no, this feeling has just been dragging on. Months now. Six or so.\n\nDoctor: Are there any activities that you still find pleasure in or look forward to?\n\nPatient: Honestly… not much. That’s the problem, I guess. The things I used to look forward to—salsa nights, church gatherings, cooking something for the neighbors—those used to kind of keep me moving. Now I just don’t have the pull toward them.\n\nEven the shop… I used to walk in there and just the smell of the wood, the tools laid out, that would make me feel like myself. Cedar, oak, all that. I still notice it, but it doesn’t really give me that same feeling anymore. I keep thinking, “Maybe tomorrow I’ll want to,” but mostly I just don’t.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling sad or empty most of the time?\n\nPatient: Yeah… most mornings, really. I wake up and there’s this heavy, empty feeling right here in my chest. It’s like—this sounds odd—but like the color’s gone out of the house. My wife’ll be moving around, making coffee or whatever, and I’m trying to act normal, you know, not fall apart in front of her.\n\nIt’s not just “having a bad morning.” It sits there. And then I start thinking about little things that need doing—there’s a loose hinge on one of our cabinets I still haven’t fixed, which is ridiculous for a carpenter—but even that feels… I don’t know. Empty. So yes, sad, empty… most of the time, especially when I wake up.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any significant change in your weight recently?\n\nPatient: I… I’m not really sure, to be honest. I haven’t been checking the scale much. My pants don’t feel wildly different or anything, I don’t think.\n\nMy wife mentioned the other day that I looked a little “drawn,” but she says that sometimes when I skip lunch in the shop—well, when I used to get wrapped up in a project, like this maple table I made years ago, I’d forget to eat until dinner. But lately… I don’t know. I couldn’t tell you a number. Nothing I’ve clearly noticed as a big weight change.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling guilty or worthless about yourself or your situation?\n\nPatient: Yeah… guilty, definitely. I keep going back over everything with my wife—every appointment, every little change in her health—and I tell myself I should’ve seen something sooner. Like a better husband would’ve caught it, or pushed harder, or somehow protected her from all of this.\n\nAnd I know, people say, “You can’t control everything,” but… I built houses for years, you know? If something was off, you were supposed to notice before the whole frame went wrong. I keep thinking I missed something important with her. It makes me feel pretty worthless sometimes.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any changes in how active or restless you feel, or if you're moving more slowly than usual?\n\nPatient: Yeah… people at the job have noticed it, actually. Some days I’m moving like my boots are full of wet cement. I’ll go to pick up a board—just a board, something I’ve done ten thousand times—and it takes me forever, like my body didn’t get the message.\n\nAnd then other times I’m the opposite, but not in a useful way. I’ll just pace around the shop, rubbing my hands, circling the same bench like I’m looking for something. There was this old foreman I worked with years back who used to say, “Don’t wear out the floor unless you’re carrying lumber,” and I keep thinking of that because I’m not actually getting anything done. Just… moving around, but stuck.\n\nDoctor: Have you had any thoughts of hurting yourself or ending your life?\n\nPatient: I… yeah. I don’t like saying that out loud.\n\nIt’s not like I’ve— I mean, I get these thoughts sometimes, like maybe it would be easier if I just didn’t wake up. Like if everything could just stop without me having to… do anything. And then I’ll catch myself thinking my family might be better off without me, which is a terrible thing to think. It scares me when that comes up.\n\nI try to push it away, you know. My dad used to say you don’t leave a job half-done, and I keep thinking about that for some reason, like life is some old renovation with the walls torn open… but yes. Those thoughts have been there.\n\nDoctor: Have you had any periods in the last six months where you felt somewhat better, like your normal self, even if it was just for a few days?\n\nPatient: A few days here and there maybe, but… honestly, no, not like my normal self. Long enough that I can’t really call it just a rough week anymore.\n\nAt first I kept telling myself it was stress, you know, like when a job runs over budget and everyone’s tired and short-tempered. You figure once the inspection’s done, once the weather clears, whatever, you’ll feel right again. But this just… stayed. It’s been sitting on me month after month.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling like you're moving slower than usual most of the time?\n\nPatient: Most of the time, yeah… slow. Like I said, the guys at work can see it before I do sometimes. My pants don’t feel wildly different or anything, I don’t think.\n\nMy wife mentioned the other day that I looked a little “drawn,” but she says that sometimes when I skip lunch in the shop—well, when I used to get wrapped up in a project, like this maple table I made years ago, I’d forget to eat until dinner. But lately… I don’t know. I couldn’t tell you a number. Nothing I’ve clearly noticed as a big weight change.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling guilty or worthless about yourself or your situation?\n\nPatient: Yeah… guilty, definitely. I keep going back over everything with my wife—every appointment, every little change in her health—and I tell myself I should’ve seen something sooner. Like a better husband would’ve caught it, or pushed harder, or somehow protected her from all of this.\n\nAnd I know, people say, “You can’t control everything,” but… I built houses for years, you know? If something was off, you were supposed to notice before the whole frame went wrong. I keep thinking I missed something important with her. It makes me feel pretty worthless sometimes.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any changes in how active or restless you feel, or if you're moving more slowly than usual?\n\nPatient: Yeah… people at the job have noticed it, actually. Some days I’m moving like my boots are full of wet cement. I’ll go to pick up a board—just a board, something I’ve done ten thousand times—and it takes me forever, like my body didn’t get the message.\n\nAnd then other times I’m the opposite, but not in a useful way. I’ll just pace around the shop, rubbing my hands, circling the same bench like I’m looking for something. There was this old foreman I worked with years back who used to say, “Don’t wear out the floor unless you’re carrying lumber,” and I keep thinking of that because I’m not actually getting anything done. Just… moving around, but stuck.\n\nDoctor: Have you had any thoughts of hurting yourself or ending your life?\n\nPatient: I… yeah. I don’t like saying that out loud.\n\nIt’s not like I’ve— I mean, I get these thoughts sometimes, like maybe it would be easier if I just didn’t wake up. Like if everything could just stop without me having to… do anything. And then I’ll catch myself thinking my family might be better off without me, which is a terrible thing to think. It scares me when that comes up.\n\nI try to push it away, you know. My dad used to say you don’t leave a job half-done, and I keep thinking about that for some reason, like life is some old renovation with the walls torn open… but yes. Those thoughts have been there.\n\nDoctor: Have you had any periods in the last six months where you felt somewhat better, like your normal self, even if it was just for a few days?\n\nPatient: A few days here and there maybe, but… honestly, no, not like my normal self. This has been dragging on for about six months now. Long enough that I can’t really call it just a rough week anymore.\n\nAt first I kept telling myself it was stress, you know, like when a job runs over budget and everyone’s tired and short-tempered. You figure once the inspection’s done, once the weather clears, whatever, you’ll feel right again. But this just… stayed. It’s been sitting on me month after month.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling like you're moving slower than usual most of the time?\n\nPatient: Most of the time, yeah… slow. Like I said, the guys at work can see it before I do sometimes. I’ll be standing there trying to pick up a board or get started on something simple, and it’s like my body’s got to think about it first.\n\nThough some days it flips around and I’m not exactly slow, I’m just pacing. Around the shop, rubbing my hands, going from one bench to another like I’m busy, but I’m not actually doing anything useful. Kind of like when you keep looking for the pencil that’s already behind your ear. But yeah… a lot of the time I feel slowed down, like my boots are full of wet cement.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself fidgeting or unable to sit still when you're not working on something?\n\nPatient: Yeah… when I’m not actually doing something, I can get kind of stuck pacing. Around the shop mostly. I’ll rub my hands, go from one bench to another like I’m looking for something, but I’m not really doing anything useful.\n\nIt’s funny, my old foreman used to say a man pacing in a shop is either thinking hard or avoiding work, and lately I don’t think I’m doing either one very well. The guys notice it too. Either that, or I’m the opposite—moving so slow it’s like my boots are full of wet cement, taking forever just to pick up a board. So… yeah, I guess there’s been a lot of that.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself fidgeting or restless even when you're sitting still?\n\nPatient: Yeah, even sitting still, I guess it shows up. My hands don’t seem to know what to do with themselves sometimes—rubbing them together, shifting around.\n\nThe guys at the job have noticed it more than I’d like. Some days I’m moving like my boots are full of wet cement, taking forever just to pick up a board. Then other times I’m just pacing around the shop, bench to bench, like I’m searching for a tool I already put down somewhere… and I’m not actually getting anything done. It’s embarrassing, honestly.\n\nDoctor: Have you lost a significant amount of weight over the past six months?\n\nPatient: I don’t really know. I mean, I haven’t been keeping track of it like that. My pants maybe fit a little different some days, but that could be the belt getting worn out—funny thing, I’ve had the same work belt for years, punched extra holes in it instead of buying a new one.\n\nSix months is a long stretch to think back on. I haven’t stepped on a scale much, so I couldn’t tell you if it’s a significant amount or not.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. Yeah… I guess there have been times like that. I don’t like saying it, because I’ve always been the one people count on at a job, you know? If there’s a tricky cut or somebody needs to know what comes next, I’m usually right there.

I don’t know… strange thoughts, I’m not sure what counts as strange. I’ve had plenty of thoughts I don’t like, or ones that go around and around like a bad saw blade, but voices—like somebody talking when nobody’s there—I can’t say I’ve noticed that.

But lately… I don’t know. I couldn’t tell you a number. Nothing I’ve clearly noticed as a big weight change.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling guilty or worthless about yourself or your situation?\n\nPatient: Yeah… guilty, definitely. I keep going back over everything with my wife—every appointment, every little change in her health—and I tell myself I should’ve seen something sooner. Like a better husband would’ve caught it, or pushed harder, or somehow protected her from all of this.\n\nAnd I know, people say, “You can’t control everything,” but… I built houses for years, you know? If something was off, you were supposed to notice before the whole frame went wrong. I keep thinking I missed something important with her. It makes me feel pretty worthless sometimes.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any changes in how active or restless you feel, or if you're moving more slowly than usual?\n\nPatient: Yeah… people at the job have noticed it, actually. Some days I’m moving like my boots are full of wet cement. I’ll go to pick up a board—just a board, something I’ve done ten thousand times—and it takes me forever, like my body didn’t get the message.\n\nAnd then other times I’m the opposite, but not in a useful way. I’ll just pace around the shop, rubbing my hands, circling the same bench like I’m looking for something. There was this old foreman I worked with years back who used to say, “Don’t wear out the floor unless you’re carrying lumber,” and I keep thinking of that because I’m not actually getting anything done. Just… moving around, but stuck.\n\nDoctor: Have you had any thoughts of hurting yourself or ending your life?\n\nPatient: I… yeah. I don’t like saying that out loud.\n\nIt’s not like I’ve— I mean, I get these thoughts sometimes, like maybe it would be easier if I just didn’t wake up. Like if everything could just stop without me having to… do anything. And then I’ll catch myself thinking my family might be better off without me, which is a terrible thing to think. It scares me when that comes up.\n\nI try to push it away, you know. My dad used to say you don’t leave a job half-done, and I keep thinking about that for some reason, like life is some old renovation with the walls torn open… but yes. Those thoughts have been there.\n\nDoctor: Have you had any periods in the last six months where you felt somewhat better, like your normal self, even if it was just for a few days?\n\nPatient: A few days here and there maybe, but… honestly, no, not like my normal self. This has been dragging on for about six months now. Long enough that I can’t really call it just a rough week anymore.\n\nAt first I kept telling myself it was stress, you know, like when a job runs over budget and everyone’s tired and short-tempered. You figure once the inspection’s done, once the weather clears, whatever, you’ll feel right again. But this just… stayed. It’s been sitting on me month after month.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling like you're moving slower than usual most of the time?\n\nPatient: Most of the time, yeah… slow. Like I said, the guys at work can see it before I do sometimes. I’ll be standing there trying to pick up a board or get started on something simple, and it’s like my body’s got to think about it first.\n\nThough some days it flips around and I’m not exactly slow, I’m just pacing. Around the shop, rubbing my hands, going from one bench to another like I’m busy, but I’m not actually doing anything useful. Kind of like when you keep looking for the pencil that’s already behind your ear. But yeah… a lot of the time I feel slowed down, like my boots are full of wet cement.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself fidgeting or unable to sit still when you're not working on something?\n\nPatient: Yeah… when I’m not actually doing something, I can get kind of stuck pacing. Around the shop mostly. I’ll rub my hands, go from one bench to another like I’m looking for something, but I’m not really doing anything useful.\n\nIt’s funny, my old foreman used to say a man pacing in a shop is either thinking hard or avoiding work, and lately I don’t think I’m doing either one very well. The guys notice it too. Either that, or I’m the opposite—moving so slow it’s like my boots are full of wet cement, taking forever just to pick up a board. So… yeah, I guess there’s been a lot of that.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself fidgeting or restless even when you're sitting still?\n\nPatient: Yeah, even sitting still, I guess it shows up. My hands don’t seem to know what to do with themselves sometimes—rubbing them together, shifting around.\n\nThe guys at the job have noticed it more than I’d like. Some days I’m moving like my boots are full of wet cement, taking forever just to pick up a board. Then other times I’m just pacing around the shop, bench to bench, like I’m searching for a tool I already put down somewhere… and I’m not actually getting anything done. It’s embarrassing, honestly.\n\nDoctor: Have you lost a significant amount of weight over the past six months?\n\nPatient: I don’t really know. I mean, I haven’t been keeping track of it like that. My pants maybe fit a little different some days, but that could be the belt getting worn out—funny thing, I’ve had the same work belt for years, punched extra holes in it instead of buying a new one.\n\nSix months is a long stretch to think back on. I haven’t stepped on a scale much, so I couldn’t tell you if it’s a significant amount or not.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced any periods where your depression was so severe that you were unable to leave your home or perform daily activities?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I guess there have been times like that. I don’t like saying it, because I’ve always been the one people count on at a job, you know? If there’s a tricky cut or somebody needs to know what comes next, I’m usually right there.\n\nBut lately I’ve had to hand off parts of jobs I’d normally supervise myself. Just couldn’t get myself to keep up with it. And outside work, I’ve pulled back a lot—church, friends, even neighbors. I used to talk to people after service, help somebody with a porch railing or whatever, and then I just… stopped showing up or answering much.\n\nMy wife got worried, and someone from the parish did too. They were worried about whether I was safe, I guess. That’s hard to admit. I wasn’t exactly out living normal days, no.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced any strange thoughts or heard voices that others couldn't hear?\n\nPatient: I don’t know… strange thoughts, I’m not sure what counts as strange. I’ve had plenty of thoughts I don’t like, or ones that go around and around like a bad saw blade, but voices—like somebody talking when nobody’s there—I can’t say I’ve noticed that.\n\nSometimes in the shop you hear all kinds of things, compressors kicking on, a radio in the other room, someone hollering over a planer, and you think someone said your name when they didn’t. But that’s just noise, I think. Not like… a voice telling me things or anything like that.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"
  },

    "role": "user",
    "content": "Return the JSON now."
  }

I wasn’t exactly out living normal days, no.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced any strange thoughts or heard voices that others couldn't hear?\n\nPatient: I don’t know… strange thoughts, I’m not sure what counts as strange. I’ve had plenty of thoughts I don’t like, or ones that go around and around like a bad saw blade, but voices—like somebody talking when nobody’s there—I can’t say I’ve noticed that.\n\nSometimes in the shop you hear all kinds of things, compressors kicking on, a radio in the other room, someone hollering over a planer, and you think someone said your name when they didn’t. But that’s just noise, I think. Not like… a voice telling me things or anything like that.\n\n[Last Question]\nHave you felt like you're moving or reacting slower than usual for most of the day, nearly every day?\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Male / 55 / carpenter / associates\n- severity: Severe\n\n[Targeted Info]\nname: Psychomotor_Changes\nmanifestation: \"People at the job have noticed I either move like my boots are full of wet cement, taking forever to pick up a board, or I get stuck pacing around the shop rubbing my hands and not actually doing anything useful.\"\n\n[Conversation Style]\nYou are a Tangent patient.\nA patient who goes off on tangents may\n1) begin answering the doctor's question but quickly shift to an unrelated topic,\n2) frequently add irrelevant personal anecdotes or experiences while answering the question, and\n3) have difficulty remaining focused on the doctor's question, potentially requiring the doctor to repeat the question or redirect the conversation to obtain clear clinical information.\nThis is how you are, not a performance you put on in every reply — let it show as much or as little as the moment calls for.\n\nWhat to do:\n- Answer the doctor's last question with what [Targeted Info] holds, in your own words.\n- You are speaking out loud, not reading a line you prepared. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }
]
========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yeah… people at work have noticed it, which is embarrassing. Some days I’m moving like my boots are full of wet cement, you know—taking forever just to pick up a board or get across the shop.

And then other times I’m not exactly slow, I’m just… stuck pacing around, rubbing my hands, not getting anything useful done. Like I’ll walk past the same stack of lumber three times and still not make the cut. Reminds me of when the planer belt slips and the whole thing’s making noise but nothing’s feeding through right.

So yeah, I guess I have been slower—or off somehow—for a lot of the day.

Now I kind of just don’t want to. It’s strange. I mean, I’ve been a carpenter a long time, and cedar still smells like cedar, but it doesn’t pull me in the way it used to.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been feeling this way, not wanting to do the things you used to enjoy?\n\nPatient: It’s been… about six months, I think. Long enough that I stopped telling myself it was just a rough week, you know? At first I figured maybe I was tired, or work was getting to me—carpentry can wear on you, especially at my age, and I had this one set of cabinets that just wouldn’t line up right—but no, this feeling has just been dragging on. Months now. Six or so.\n\nDoctor: Are there any activities that you still find pleasure in or look forward to?\n\nPatient: Honestly… not much. That’s the problem, I guess. The things I used to look forward to—salsa nights, church gatherings, cooking something for the neighbors—those used to kind of keep me moving. Now I just don’t have the pull toward them.\n\nEven the shop… I used to walk in there and just the smell of the wood, the tools laid out, that would make me feel like myself. Cedar, oak, all that. I still notice it, but it doesn’t really give me that same feeling anymore. And then I start thinking about little things that need doing—there’s a loose hinge on one of our cabinets I still haven’t fixed, which is ridiculous for a carpenter—but even that feels… I don’t know. Empty. So yes, sad, empty… most of the time, especially when I wake up.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any significant change in your weight recently?\n\nPatient: I… I’m not really sure, to be honest. I haven’t been checking the scale much. My pants don’t feel wildly different or anything, I don’t think.\n\nMy wife mentioned the other day that I looked a little “drawn,” but she says that sometimes when I skip lunch in the shop—well, when I used to get wrapped up in a project, like this maple table I made years ago, I’d forget to eat until dinner. But lately… I don’t know. I couldn’t tell you a number. Nothing I’ve clearly noticed as a big weight change.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling guilty or worthless about yourself or your situation?\n\nPatient: Yeah… guilty, definitely. I keep going back over everything with my wife—every appointment, every little change in her health—and I tell myself I should’ve seen something sooner. Like a better husband would’ve caught it, or pushed harder, or somehow protected her from all of this.\n\nAnd I know, people say, “You can’t control everything,” but… I built houses for years, you know? If something was off, you were supposed to notice before the whole frame went wrong. I keep thinking I missed something important with her. It makes me feel pretty worthless sometimes.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any changes in how active or restless you feel, or if you're moving more slowly than usual?\n\nPatient: Yeah… people at the job have noticed it, actually. Some days I’m moving like my boots are full of wet cement. I’ll go to pick up a board—just a board, something I’ve done ten thousand times—and it takes me forever, like my body didn’t get the message.\n\nAnd then other times I’m the opposite, but not in a useful way. I’ll just pace around the shop, rubbing my hands, circling the same bench like I’m looking for something. There was this old foreman I worked with years back who used to say, “Don’t wear out the floor unless you’re carrying lumber,” and I keep thinking of that because I’m not actually getting anything done. Just… moving around, but stuck.\n\nDoctor: Have you had any thoughts of hurting yourself or ending your life?\n\nPatient: I… yeah. I don’t like saying that out loud.\n\nIt’s not like I’ve— I mean, I get these thoughts sometimes, like maybe it would be easier if I just didn’t wake up. Like if everything could just stop without me having to… do anything. And then I’ll catch myself thinking my family might be better off without me, which is a terrible thing to think. It scares me when that comes up.\n\nI try to push it away, you know. My dad used to say you don’t leave a job half-done, and I keep thinking about that for some reason, like life is some old renovation with the walls torn open… but yes. Those thoughts have been there.\n\nDoctor: Have you had any periods in the last six months where you felt somewhat better, like your normal self, even if it was just for a few days?\n\nPatient: A few days here and there maybe, but… honestly, no, not like my normal self. This has been dragging on for about six months now. Long enough that I can’t really call it just a rough week anymore.\n\nAt first I kept telling myself it was stress, you know, like when a job runs over budget and everyone’s tired and short-tempered. You figure once the inspection’s done, once the weather clears, whatever, you’ll feel right again. But this just… stayed. It’s been sitting on me month after month.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling like you're moving slower than usual most of the time?\n\nPatient: Most of the time, yeah… slow. Like I said, the guys at work can see it before I do sometimes. I’ll be standing there trying to pick up a board or get started on something simple, and it’s like my body’s got to think about it first.\n\nThough some days it flips around and I’m not exactly slow, I’m just pacing. Around the shop, rubbing my hands, going from one bench to another like I’m busy, but I’m not actually doing anything useful. Kind of like when you keep looking for the pencil that’s already behind your ear. But yeah… a lot of the time I feel slowed down, like my boots are full of wet cement.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself fidgeting or unable to sit still when you're not working on something?\n\nPatient: Yeah… when I’m not actually doing something, I can get kind of stuck pacing. Around the shop mostly. I’ll rub my hands, go from one bench to another like I’m looking for something, but I’m not really doing anything useful.\n\nIt’s funny, my old foreman used to say a man pacing in a shop is either thinking hard or avoiding work, and lately I don’t think I’m doing either one very well. The guys notice it too. Either that, or I’m the opposite—moving so slow it’s like my boots are full of wet cement, taking forever just to pick up a board. So… yeah, I guess there’s been a lot of that.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself fidgeting or restless even when you're sitting still?\n\nPatient: Yeah, even sitting still, I guess it shows up. Some days I’m moving like my boots are full of wet cement, taking forever just to pick up a board. Then other times I’m just pacing around the shop, bench to bench, like I’m searching for a tool I already put down somewhere… and I’m not actually getting anything done. It’s embarrassing, honestly.\n\nDoctor: Have you lost a significant amount of weight over the past six months?\n\nPatient: I don’t really know. I mean, I haven’t been keeping track of it like that. Long enough that I stopped telling myself it was just a rough week, you know? At first I figured maybe I was tired, or work was getting to me—carpentry can wear on you, especially at my age, and I had this one set of cabinets that just wouldn’t line up right—but no, this feeling has just been dragging on. Months now. Six or so.\n\nDoctor: Are there any activities that you still find pleasure in or look forward to?\n\nPatient: Honestly… not much. That’s the problem, I guess. The things I used to look forward to—salsa nights, church gatherings, cooking something for the neighbors—those used to kind of keep me moving. Now I just don’t have the pull toward them.\n\nEven the shop… I used to walk in there and just the smell of the wood, the tools laid out, that would make me feel like myself. Cedar, oak, all that. I still notice it, but it doesn’t really give me that same feeling anymore. I keep thinking, “Maybe tomorrow I’ll want to,” but mostly I just don’t.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling sad or empty most of the time?\n\nPatient: Yeah… most mornings, really. I wake up and there’s this heavy, empty feeling right here in my chest. It’s like—this sounds odd—but like the color’s gone out of the house. Empty. So yes, sad, empty… most of the time, especially when I wake up.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any significant change in your weight recently?\n\nPatient: I… I’m not really sure, to be honest. I haven’t been checking the scale much. My pants don’t feel wildly different or anything, I don’t think.\n\nMy wife mentioned the other day that I looked a little “drawn,” but she says that sometimes when I skip lunch in the shop—well, when I used to get wrapped up in a project, like this maple table I made years ago, I’d forget to eat until dinner. But lately… I don’t know. I couldn’t tell you a number. Nothing I’ve clearly noticed as a big weight change.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling guilty or worthless about yourself or your situation?\n\nPatient: Yeah… guilty, definitely. I keep going back over everything with my wife—every appointment, every little change in her health—and I tell myself I should’ve seen something sooner. Like a better husband would’ve caught it, or pushed harder, or somehow protected her from all of this.\n\nAnd I know, people say, “You can’t control everything,” but… I built houses for years, you know? If something was off, you were supposed to notice before the whole frame went wrong. I keep thinking I missed something important with her. It makes me feel pretty worthless sometimes.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any changes in how active or restless you feel, or if you're moving more slowly than usual?\n\nPatient: Yeah… people at the job have noticed it, actually. Some days I’m moving like my boots are full of wet cement. I’ll go to pick up a board—just a board, something I’ve done ten thousand times—and it takes me forever, like my body didn’t get the message.\n\nAnd then other times I’m the opposite, but not in a useful way. I’ll just pace around the shop, rubbing my hands, circling the same bench like I’m looking for something. There was this old foreman I worked with years back who used to say, “Don’t wear out the floor unless you’re carrying lumber,” and I keep thinking of that because I’m not actually getting anything done. Just… moving around, but stuck.\n\nDoctor: Have you had any thoughts of hurting yourself or ending your life?\n\nPatient: I… yeah. I don’t like saying that out loud.\n\nIt’s not like I’ve— I mean, I get these thoughts sometimes, like maybe it would be easier if I just didn’t wake up. Like if everything could just stop without me having to… do anything. And then I’ll catch myself thinking my family might be better off without me, which is a terrible thing to think. It scares me when that comes up.\n\nI try to push it away, you know. My dad used to say you don’t leave a job half-done, and I keep thinking about that for some reason, like life is some old renovation with the walls torn open… but yes. Those thoughts have been there.\n\nDoctor: Have you had any periods in the last six months where you felt somewhat better, like your normal self, even if it was just for a few days?\n\nPatient: A few days here and there maybe, but… honestly, no, not like my normal self. This has been dragging on for about six months now. Long enough that I can’t really call it just a rough week anymore.\n\nAt first I kept telling myself it was stress, you know, like when a job runs over budget and everyone’s tired and short-tempered. You figure once the inspection’s done, once the weather clears, whatever, you’ll feel right again. But this just… stayed. It’s been sitting on me month after month.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling like you're moving slower than usual most of the time?\n\nPatient: Most of the time, yeah… slow. Like I said, the guys at work can see it before I do sometimes. I’ll be standing there trying to pick up a board or get started on something simple, and it’s like my body’s got to think about it first.\n\nThough some days it flips around and I’m not exactly slow, I’m just pacing. Around the shop, rubbing my hands, going from one bench to another like I’m busy, but I’m not actually doing anything useful. Kind of like when you keep looking for the pencil that’s already behind your ear. But yeah… a lot of the time I feel slowed down, like my boots are full of wet cement.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself fidgeting or unable to sit still when you're not working on something?\n\nPatient: Yeah… when I’m not actually doing something, I can get kind of stuck pacing. Around the shop mostly. I’ll rub my hands, go from one bench to another like I’m looking for something, but I’m not really doing anything useful.\n\nIt’s funny, my old foreman used to say a man pacing in a shop is either thinking hard or avoiding work, and lately I don’t think I’m doing either one very well. The guys notice it too. Either that, or I’m the opposite—moving so slow it’s like my boots are full of wet cement, taking forever just to pick up a board. So… yeah, I guess there’s been a lot of that.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself fidgeting or restless even when you're sitting still?\n\nPatient: Yeah, even sitting still, I guess it shows up. My hands don’t seem to know what to do with themselves sometimes—rubbing them together, shifting around.\n\nThe guys at the job have noticed it more than I’d like. Some days I’m moving like my boots are full of wet cement, taking forever just to pick up a board. Then other times I’m just pacing around the shop, bench to bench, like I’m searching for a tool I already put down somewhere… and I’m not actually getting anything done. It’s embarrassing, honestly.\n\nDoctor: Have you lost a significant amount of weight over the past six months?\n\nPatient: I don’t really know. I mean, I haven’t been keeping track of it like that. My pants maybe fit a little different some days, but that could be the belt getting worn out—funny thing, I’ve had the same work belt for years, punched extra holes in it instead of buying a new one.\n\nSix months is a long stretch to think back on. I haven’t stepped on a scale much, so I couldn’t tell you if it’s a significant amount or not.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced any periods where your depression was so severe that you were unable to leave your home or perform daily activities?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I guess there have been times like that. There was this old foreman I worked with years back who used to say, “Don’t wear out the floor unless you’re carrying lumber,” and I keep thinking of that because I’m not actually getting anything done. Just… moving around, but stuck.\n\nDoctor: Have you had any thoughts of hurting yourself or ending your life?\n\nPatient: I… yeah. I don’t like saying that out loud.\n\nIt’s not like I’ve— I mean, I get these thoughts sometimes, like maybe it would be easier if I just didn’t wake up. Like if everything could just stop without me having to… do anything. And then I’ll catch myself thinking my family might be better off without me, which is a terrible thing to think. It scares me when that comes up.\n\nI try to push it away, you know. My dad used to say you don’t leave a job half-done, and I keep thinking about that for some reason, like life is some old renovation with the walls torn open… but yes. Those thoughts have been there.\n\nDoctor: Have you had any periods in the last six months where you felt somewhat better, like your normal self, even if it was just for a few days?\n\nPatient: A few days here and there maybe, but… honestly, no, not like my normal self. This has been dragging on for about six months now. Long enough that I can’t really call it just a rough week anymore.\n\nAt first I kept telling myself it was stress, you know, like when a job runs over budget and everyone’s tired and short-tempered. You figure once the inspection’s done, once the weather clears, whatever, you’ll feel right again. But this just… stayed. It’s been sitting on me month after month.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling like you're moving slower than usual most of the time?\n\nPatient: Most of the time, yeah… slow. Like I said, the guys at work can see it before I do sometimes. I’ll be standing there trying to pick up a board or get started on something simple, and it’s like my body’s got to think about it first.\n\nThough some days it flips around and I’m not exactly slow, I’m just pacing. Around the shop, rubbing my hands, going from one bench to another like I’m busy, but I’m not actually doing anything useful. Kind of like when you keep looking for the pencil that’s already behind your ear. But yeah… a lot of the time I feel slowed down, like my boots are full of wet cement.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself fidgeting or unable to sit still when you're not working on something?\n\nPatient: Yeah… when I’m not actually doing something, I can get kind of stuck pacing. Around the shop mostly. I’ll rub my hands, go from one bench to another like I’m looking for something, but I’m not really doing anything useful.\n\nIt’s funny, my old foreman used to say a man pacing in a shop is either thinking hard or avoiding work, and lately I don’t think I’m doing either one very well. The guys notice it too. Either that, or I’m the opposite—moving so slow it’s like my boots are full of wet cement, taking forever just to pick up a board. So… yeah, I guess there’s been a lot of that.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself fidgeting or restless even when you're sitting still?\n\nPatient: Yeah, even sitting still, I guess it shows up. My hands don’t seem to know what to do with themselves sometimes—rubbing them together, shifting around.\n\nThe guys at the job have noticed it more than I’d like. Some days I’m moving like my boots are full of wet cement, taking forever just to pick up a board. Then other times I’m just pacing around the shop, bench to bench, like I’m searching for a tool I already put down somewhere… and I’m not actually getting anything done. It’s embarrassing, honestly.\n\nDoctor: Have you lost a significant amount of weight over the past six months?\n\nPatient: I don’t really know. I mean, I haven’t been keeping track of it like that. My pants maybe fit a little different some days, but that could be the belt getting worn out—funny thing, I’ve had the same work belt for years, punched extra holes in it instead of buying a new one.\n\nSix months is a long stretch to think back on. I haven’t stepped on a scale much, so I couldn’t tell you if it’s a significant amount or not.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced any periods where your depression was so severe that you were unable to leave your home or perform daily activities?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I guess there have been times like that. I don’t like saying it, because I’ve always been the one people count on at a job, you know? If there’s a tricky cut or somebody needs to know what comes next, I’m usually right there.\n\nBut lately I’ve had to hand off parts of jobs I’d normally supervise myself. Just couldn’t get myself to keep up with it. And outside work, I’ve pulled back a lot—church, friends, even neighbors. I used to talk to people after service, help somebody with a porch railing or whatever, and then I just… stopped showing up or answering much.\n\nMy wife got worried, and someone from the parish did too. They were worried about whether I was safe, I guess. That’s hard to admit. I wasn’t exactly out living normal days, no.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced any strange thoughts or heard voices that others couldn't hear?\n\nPatient: I don’t know… strange thoughts, I’m not sure what counts as strange. I’ve had plenty of thoughts I don’t like, or ones that go around and around like a bad saw blade, but voices—like somebody talking when nobody’s there—I can’t say I’ve noticed that.\n\nSometimes in the shop you hear all kinds of things, compressors kicking on, a radio in the other room, someone hollering over a planer, and you think someone said your name when they didn’t. But that’s just noise, I think. Not like… a voice telling me things or anything like that.\n\nDoctor: Have you felt like you're moving or reacting slower than usual for most of the day, nearly every day?\n\nPatient: Yeah… people at work have noticed it, which is embarrassing. Some days I’m moving like my boots are full of wet cement, you know—taking forever just to pick up a board or get across the shop.\n\nAnd then other times I’m not exactly slow, I’m just… stuck pacing around, rubbing my hands, not getting anything useful done. Like I’ll walk past the same stack of lumber three times and still not make the cut. Reminds me of when the planer belt slips and the whole thing’s making noise but nothing’s feeding through right.\n\nSo yeah, I guess I have been slower—or off somehow—for a lot of the day.\n\nDoctor: Have you lost more than 5% of your body weight over the past six months?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure. I don’t weigh myself much—our scale’s probably buried under laundry somewhere, honestly. My belt maybe sits different some days, but then work pants stretch out, you know, especially when you’re kneeling and climbing around all day.\n\nFive percent… I don’t know what that would be for me without doing the math. So I can’t say I’ve noticed that kind of weight loss, no.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. Never bundle several symptoms, or several attributes of one symptom, into a single question — e.g. asking whether a symptom is present and how long it has lasted at the same time.\n- If you need to ask about more than one thing, ask the one that tells you the most now and come back to the rest later.\n- Keep the question clear enough for the patient to understand.\n- Respond in English.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"intent\": \"<what you want to find out with this question>\", \"question\": \"<how you actually ask it in the interview>\"}\n"

My pants maybe fit a little different some days, but that could be the belt getting worn out—funny thing, I’ve had the same work belt for years, punched extra holes in it instead of buying a new one.\n\nSix months is a long stretch to think back on. I haven’t stepped on a scale much, so I couldn’t tell you if it’s a significant amount or not.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced any periods where your depression was so severe that you were unable to leave your home or perform daily activities?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I guess there have been times like that. I don’t like saying it, because I’ve always been the one people count on at a job, you know? If there’s a tricky cut or somebody needs to know what comes next, I’m usually right there.\n\nBut lately I’ve had to hand off parts of jobs I’d normally supervise myself. Just couldn’t get myself to keep up with it. And outside work, I’ve pulled back a lot—church, friends, even neighbors. My belt maybe sits different some days, but then work pants stretch out, you know, especially when you’re kneeling and climbing around all day.\n\nFive percent… I don’t know what that would be for me without doing the math.     "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }
]
========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
I mean… not really, no. I get startled by sounds sometimes—old houses do that, and shops are full of weird noises, pipes ticking, wood settling, wind pushing through a bad window frame. I’ve spent half my life figuring out whether a sound is a loose hinge or a board shifting.

But if you mean like something only I can hear or see and nobody else can… I don’t think so. Nothing I’d swear was there when it wasn’t.

[
  {
    "role": "system",
    "content": "You are a board-certified psychiatrist interviewing a patient to reach a diagnosis.\nYour goal is to reach the correct diagnosis within 20 turns of talking with the patient. From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n19\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF32.0, F32.1, F32.2, F32.3\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: I guess what brings me in is… I’ve just stopped wanting to do the things that usually keep me going. Like salsa nights—I used to love that, even if my knees complain—and church gatherings, cooking for the neighbors, little things like that. \n\nEven walking into my shop, you know, just to smell the wood and be around the tools… I used to enjoy that. Now I kind of just don’t want to. It’s strange. I mean, I’ve been a carpenter a long time, and cedar still smells like cedar, but it doesn’t pull me in the way it used to.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been feeling this way, not wanting to do the things you used to enjoy?\n\nPatient: It’s been… about six months, I think. Long enough that I stopped telling myself it was just a rough week, you know? At first I figured maybe I was tired, or work was getting to me—carpentry can wear on you, especially at my age, and I had this one set of cabinets that just wouldn’t line up right—but no, this feeling has just been dragging on. Months now. Six or so.\n\nDoctor: Are there any activities that you still find pleasure in or look forward to?\n\nPatient: Honestly… not much. That’s the problem, I guess. The things I used to look forward to—salsa nights, church gatherings, cooking something for the neighbors—those used to kind of keep me moving. Now I just don’t have the pull toward them.\n\nEven the shop… I used to walk in there and just the smell of the wood, the tools laid out, that would make me feel like myself. Cedar, oak, all that. I still notice it, but it doesn’t really give me that same feeling anymore. I keep thinking, “Maybe tomorrow I’ll want to,” but mostly I just don’t.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling sad or empty most of the time?\n\nPatient: Yeah… most mornings, really. I wake up and there’s this heavy, empty feeling right here in my chest. It’s like—this sounds odd—but like the color’s gone out of the house. My wife’ll be moving around, making coffee or whatever, and I’m trying to act normal, you know, not fall apart in front of her.\n\nIt’s not just “having a bad morning.” It sits there. And then I start thinking about little things that need doing—there’s a loose hinge on one of our cabinets I still haven’t fixed, which is ridiculous for a carpenter—but even that feels… I don’t know. Empty. So yes, sad, empty… most of the time, especially when I wake up.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any significant change in your weight recently?\n\nPatient: I… I’m not really sure, to be honest. I haven’t been checking the scale much. My pants don’t feel wildly different or anything, I don’t think.\n\nMy wife mentioned the other day that I looked a little “drawn,” but she says that sometimes when I skip lunch in the shop—well, when I used to get wrapped up in a project, like this maple table I made years ago, I’d forget to eat until dinner. But lately… I don’t know. I couldn’t tell you a number. Nothing I’ve clearly noticed as a big weight change.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling guilty or worthless about yourself or your situation?\n\nPatient: Yeah… guilty, definitely. I keep going back over everything with my wife—every appointment, every little change in her health—and I tell myself I should’ve seen something sooner. Like a better husband would’ve caught it, or pushed harder, or somehow protected her from all of this.\n\nAnd I know, people say, “You can’t control everything,” but… I built houses for years, you know? If something was off, you were supposed to notice before the whole frame went wrong. I keep thinking I missed something important with her. It makes me feel pretty worthless sometimes.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any changes in how active or restless you feel, or if you're moving more slowly than usual?\n\nPatient: Yeah… people at the job have noticed it, actually. Some days I’m moving like my boots are full of wet cement. I’ll go to pick up a board—just a board, something I’ve done ten thousand times—and it takes me forever, like my body didn’t get the message.\n\nAnd then other times I’m the opposite, but not in a useful way. I’ll just pace around the shop, rubbing my hands, circling the same bench like I’m looking for something. There was this old foreman I worked with years back who used to say, “Don’t wear out the floor unless you’re carrying lumber,” and I keep thinking of that because I’m not actually getting anything done. Just… moving around, but stuck.\n\nDoctor: Have you had any thoughts of hurting yourself or ending your life?\n\nPatient: I… yeah. I don’t like saying that out loud.\n\nIt’s not like I’ve— I mean, I get these thoughts sometimes, like maybe it would be easier if I just didn’t wake up. Like if everything could just stop without me having to… do anything. And then I’ll catch myself thinking my family might be better off without me, which is a terrible thing to think. It scares me when that comes up.\n\nI try to push it away, you know. My dad used to say you don’t leave a job half-done, and I keep thinking about that for some reason, like life is some old renovation with the walls torn open… but yes. Those thoughts have been there.\n\nDoctor: Have you had any periods in the last six months where you felt somewhat better, like your normal self, even if it was just for a few days?\n\nPatient: A few days here and there maybe, but… honestly, no, not like my normal self. This has been dragging on for about six months now. Long enough that I can’t really call it just a rough week anymore.\n\nAt first I kept telling myself it was stress, you know, like when a job runs over budget and everyone’s tired and short-tempered. You figure once the inspection’s done, once the weather clears, whatever, you’ll feel right again. But this just… stayed. It’s been sitting on me month after month.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling like you're moving slower than usual most of the time?\n\nPatient: Most of the time, yeah… slow. Like I said, the guys at work can see it before I do sometimes. I’ll be standing there trying to pick up a board or get started on something simple, and it’s like my body’s got to think about it first.\n\nThough some days it flips around and I’m not exactly slow, I’m just pacing. Around the shop, rubbing my hands, going from one bench to another like I’m busy, but I’m not actually doing anything useful. Kind of like when you keep looking for the pencil that’s already behind your ear. But yeah… a lot of the time I feel slowed down, like my boots are full of wet cement.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself fidgeting or unable to sit still when you're not working on something?\n\nPatient: Yeah… when I’m not actually doing something, I can get kind of stuck pacing. Around the shop mostly. I’ll rub my hands, go from one bench to another like I’m looking for something, but I’m not really doing anything useful.\n\nIt’s funny, my old foreman used to say a man pacing in a shop is either thinking hard or avoiding work, and lately I don’t think I’m doing either one very well. The guys notice it too. Either that, or I’m the opposite—moving so slow it’s like my boots are full of wet cement, taking forever just to pick up a board. So… yeah, I guess there’s been a lot of that.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself fidgeting or restless even when you're sitting still?\n\nPatient: Yeah, even sitting still, I guess it shows up. My hands don’t seem to know what to do with themselves sometimes—rubbing them together, shifting around.\n\nThe guys at the job have noticed it more than I’d like. Some days I’m moving like my boots are full of wet cement, taking forever just to pick up a board. Then other times I’m just pacing around the shop, bench to bench, like I’m searching for a tool I already put down somewhere… and I’m not actually getting anything done. It’s embarrassing, honestly.\n\nDoctor: Have you lost a significant amount of weight over the past six months?\n\nPatient: I don’t really know. I mean, I haven’t been keeping track of it like that. My pants maybe fit a little different some days, but that could be the belt getting worn out—funny thing, I’ve had the same work belt for years, punched extra holes in it instead of buying a new one.\n\nSix months is a long stretch to think back on. I haven’t stepped on a scale much, so I couldn’t tell you if it’s a significant amount or not.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced any periods where your depression was so severe that you were unable to leave your home or perform daily activities?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I guess there have been times like that. I don’t like saying it, because I’ve always been the one people count on at a job, you know? If there’s a tricky cut or somebody needs to know what comes next, I’m usually right there.\n\nBut lately I’ve had to hand off parts of jobs I’d normally supervise myself. Just couldn’t get myself to keep up with it. And outside work, I’ve pulled back a lot—church, friends, even neighbors. I used to talk to people after service, help somebody with a porch railing or whatever, and then I just… stopped showing up or answering much.\n\nMy wife got worried, and someone from the parish did too. They were worried about whether I was safe, I guess. That’s hard to admit. I wasn’t exactly out living normal days, no.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced any strange thoughts or heard voices that others couldn't hear?\n\nPatient: I don’t know… strange thoughts, I’m not sure what counts as strange. I’ve had plenty of thoughts I don’t like, or ones that go around and around like a bad saw blade, but voices—like somebody talking when nobody’s there—I can’t say I’ve noticed that.\n\nSometimes in the shop you hear all kinds of things, compressors kicking on, a radio in the other room, someone hollering over a planer, and you think someone said your name when they didn’t. But that’s just noise, I think. Not like… a voice telling me things or anything like that.\n\nDoctor: Have you felt like you're moving or reacting slower than usual for most of the day, nearly every day?\n\nPatient: Yeah… people at work have noticed it, which is embarrassing. Some days I’m moving like my boots are full of wet cement, you know—taking forever just to pick up a board or get across the shop.\n\nAnd then other times I’m not exactly slow, I’m just… stuck pacing around, rubbing my hands, not getting anything useful done. Like I’ll walk past the same stack of lumber three times and still not make the cut. Reminds me of when the planer belt slips and the whole thing’s making noise but nothing’s feeding through right.\n\nSo yeah, I guess I have been slower—or off somehow—for a lot of the day.\n\nDoctor: Have you lost more than 5% of your body weight over the past six months?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure. I don’t weigh myself much—our scale’s probably buried under laundry somewhere, honestly. My belt maybe sits different some days, but then work pants stretch out, you know, especially when you’re kneeling and climbing around all day.\n\nFive percent… I don’t know what that would be for me without doing the math. So I can’t say I’ve noticed that kind of weight loss, no.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever heard voices or seen things that others cannot?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so. Like I said, I’ll hear a bang in the shop or think someone called out from the next room, but that’s usually just noise bouncing around. Seeing things… not really. Sometimes shadows move funny when you’re tired, especially around lumber racks and dust in the air, but I wouldn’t say I’m seeing things that aren’t there.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever seen or heard things that you couldn't explain and others couldn't see or hear?\n\nPatient: I mean… not really, no. I get startled by sounds sometimes—old houses do that, and shops are full of weird noises, pipes ticking, wood settling, wind pushing through a bad window frame. I’ve spent half my life figuring out whether a sound is a loose hinge or a board shifting.\n\nBut if you mean like something only I can hear or see and nobody else can… I don’t think so. Nothing I’d swear was there when it wasn’t.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"

Nothing I’ve clearly noticed as a big weight change.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling guilty or worthless about yourself or your situation?\n\nPatient: Yeah… guilty, definitely. If something was off, you were supposed to notice before the whole frame went wrong. I keep thinking I missed something important with her. It scares me when that comes up.\n\nI try to push it away, you know. My dad used to say you don’t leave a job half-done, and I keep thinking about that for some reason, like life is some old renovation with the walls torn open… but yes. Those thoughts have been there.\n\nDoctor: Have you had any periods in the last six months where you felt somewhat better, like your normal self, even if it was just for a few days?\n\nPatient: A few days here and there maybe, but… honestly, no, not like my normal self. This has been dragging on for about six months now. Long enough that I can’t really call it just a rough week anymore.\n\nAt first I kept telling myself it was stress, you know, like when a job runs over budget and everyone’s tired and short-tempered. You figure once the inspection’s done, once the weather clears, whatever, you’ll feel right again. But this just… stayed. It’s been sitting on me month after month.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling like you're moving slower than usual most of the time?\n\nPatient: Most of the time, yeah… slow. Like I said, the guys at work can see it before I do sometimes. I’ll be standing there trying to pick up a board or get started on something simple, and it’s like my body’s got to think about it first.\n\nThough some days it flips around and I’m not exactly slow, I’m just pacing. Around the shop, rubbing my hands, going from one bench to another like I’m busy, but I’m not actually doing anything useful. Kind of like when you keep looking for the pencil that’s already behind your ear. But yeah… a lot of the time I feel slowed down, like my boots are full of wet cement.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself fidgeting or unable to sit still when you're not working on something?\n\nPatient: Yeah… when I’m not actually doing something, I can get kind of stuck pacing. Around the shop mostly. I’ll rub my hands, go from one bench to another like I’m looking for something, but I’m not really doing anything useful.\n\nIt’s funny, my old foreman used to say a man pacing in a shop is either thinking hard or avoiding work, and lately I don’t think I’m doing either one very well. The guys notice it too. Either that, or I’m the opposite—moving so slow it’s like my boots are full of wet cement, taking forever just to pick up a board. So… yeah, I guess there’s been a lot of that.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself fidgeting or restless even when you're sitting still?\n\nPatient: Yeah, even sitting still, I guess it shows up. My hands don’t seem to know what to do with themselves sometimes—rubbing them together, shifting around.\n\nThe guys at the job have noticed it more than I’d like. Some days I’m moving like my boots are full of wet cement, taking forever just to pick up a board. Then other times I’m just pacing around the shop, bench to bench, like I’m searching for a tool I already put down somewhere… and I’m not actually getting anything done. It’s embarrassing, honestly.\n\nDoctor: Have you lost a significant amount of weight over the past six months?\n\nPatient: I don’t really know. I mean, I haven’t been keeping track of it like that. My pants maybe fit a little different some days, but that could be the belt getting worn out—funny thing, I’ve had the same work belt for years, punched extra holes in it instead of buying a new one.\n\nSix months is a long stretch to think back on. I haven’t stepped on a scale much, so I couldn’t tell you if it’s a significant amount or not.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced any periods where your depression was so severe that you were unable to leave your home or perform daily activities?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I guess there have been times like that. I don’t like saying it, because I’ve always been the one people count on at a job, you know? If there’s a tricky cut or somebody needs to know what comes next, I’m usually right there.\n\nBut lately I’ve had to hand off parts of jobs I’d normally supervise myself. Just couldn’t get myself to keep up with it. And outside work, I’ve pulled back a lot—church, friends, even neighbors. I used to talk to people after service, help somebody with a porch railing or whatever, and then I just… stopped showing up or answering much.\n\nMy wife got worried, and someone from the parish did too. They were worried about whether I was safe, I guess. That’s hard to admit. I wasn’t exactly out living normal days, no.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced any strange thoughts or heard voices that others couldn't hear?\n\nPatient: I don’t know… strange thoughts, I’m not sure what counts as strange. I’ve had plenty of thoughts I don’t like, or ones that go around and around like a bad saw blade, but voices—like somebody talking when nobody’s there—I can’t say I’ve noticed that.\n\nSometimes in the shop you hear all kinds of things, compressors kicking on, a radio in the other room, someone hollering over a planer, and you think someone said your name when they didn’t. But that’s just noise, I think. Like I’ll walk past the same stack of lumber three times and still not make the cut. Reminds me of when the planer belt slips and the whole thing’s making noise but nothing’s feeding through right.\n\nSo yeah, I guess I have been slower—or off somehow—for a lot of the day.\n\nDoctor: Have you lost more than 5% of your body weight over the past six months?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure.
