I keep going back over everything with my wife—every appointment, every little change in her health—and I tell myself I should’ve seen something sooner. Like a better husband would’ve caught it, or pushed harder, or somehow protected her from all of this.\n\nAnd I know, people say, “You can’t control everything,” but… I built houses for years, you know? If something was off, you were supposed to notice before the whole frame went wrong. I keep thinking I missed something important with her. It makes me feel pretty worthless sometimes.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any changes in how active or restless you feel, or if you're moving more slowly than usual?\n\nPatient: Yeah… people at the job have noticed it, actually. Some days I’m moving like my boots are full of wet cement. I’ll go to pick up a board—just a board, something I’ve done ten thousand times—and it takes me forever, like my body didn’t get the message.\n\nAnd then other times I’m the opposite, but not in a useful way. I’ll just pace around the shop, rubbing my hands, circling the same bench like I’m looking for something. There was this old foreman I worked with years back who used to say, “Don’t wear out the floor unless you’re carrying lumber,” and I keep thinking of that because I’m not actually getting anything done. Just… moving around, but stuck.\n\nDoctor: Have you had any thoughts of hurting yourself or ending your life?\n\nPatient: I… yeah. I don’t like saying that out loud.\n\nIt’s not like I’ve— I mean, I get these thoughts sometimes, like maybe it would be easier if I just didn’t wake up. Like if everything could just stop without me having to… do anything. And then I’ll catch myself thinking my family might be better off without me, which is a terrible thing to think. It scares me when that comes up.\n\nI try to push it away, you know. My dad used to say you don’t leave a job half-done, and I keep thinking about that for some reason, like life is some old renovation with the walls torn open… but yes. Those thoughts have been there.\n\nDoctor: Have you had any periods in the last six months where you felt somewhat better, like your normal self, even if it was just for a few days?\n\nPatient: A few days here and there maybe, but… honestly, no, not like my normal self. This has been dragging on for about six months now. Long enough that I can’t really call it just a rough week anymore.\n\nAt first I kept telling myself it was stress, you know, like when a job runs over budget and everyone’s tired and short-tempered. You figure once the inspection’s done, once the weather clears, whatever, you’ll feel right again. But this just… stayed. It’s been sitting on me month after month.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling like you're moving slower than usual most of the time?\n\nPatient: Most of the time, yeah… slow. Like I said, the guys at work can see it before I do sometimes. I’ll be standing there trying to pick up a board or get started on something simple, and it’s like my body’s got to think about it first.\n\nThough some days it flips around and I’m not exactly slow, I’m just pacing. Around the shop, rubbing my hands, going from one bench to another like I’m busy, but I’m not actually doing anything useful. Kind of like when you keep looking for the pencil that’s already behind your ear. But yeah… a lot of the time I feel slowed down, like my boots are full of wet cement.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself fidgeting or unable to sit still when you're not working on something?\n\nPatient: Yeah… when I’m not actually doing something, I can get kind of stuck pacing. Around the shop mostly. I’ll rub my hands, go from one bench to another like I’m looking for something, but I’m not really doing anything useful.\n\nIt’s funny, my old foreman used to say a man pacing in a shop is either thinking hard or avoiding work, and lately I don’t think I’m doing either one very well. The guys notice it too. Either that, or I’m the opposite—moving so slow it’s like my boots are full of wet cement, taking forever just to pick up a board. So… yeah, I guess there’s been a lot of that.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself fidgeting or restless even when you're sitting still?\n\nPatient: Yeah, even sitting still, I guess it shows up. My hands don’t seem to know what to do with themselves sometimes—rubbing them together, shifting around.\n\nThe guys at the job have noticed it more than I’d like. Some days I’m moving like my boots are full of wet cement, taking forever just to pick up a board. Then other times I’m just pacing around the shop, bench to bench, like I’m searching for a tool I already put down somewhere… and I’m not actually getting anything done. It’s embarrassing, honestly.\n\nDoctor: Have you lost a significant amount of weight over the past six months?\n\nPatient: I don’t really know. I mean, I haven’t been keeping track of it like that. My pants maybe fit a little different some days, but that could be the belt getting worn out—funny thing, I’ve had the same work belt for years, punched extra holes in it instead of buying a new one.\n\nSix months is a long stretch to think back on. I haven’t stepped on a scale much, so I couldn’t tell you if it’s a significant amount or not.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced any periods where your depression was so severe that you were unable to leave your home or perform daily activities?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I guess there have been times like that. I don’t like saying it, because I’ve always been the one people count on at a job, you know? If there’s a tricky cut or somebody needs to know what comes next, I’m usually right there.\n\nBut lately I’ve had to hand off parts of jobs I’d normally supervise myself. Just couldn’t get myself to keep up with it. And outside work, I’ve pulled back a lot—church, friends, even neighbors. I used to talk to people after service, help somebody with a porch railing or whatever, and then I just… stopped showing up or answering much.\n\nMy wife got worried, and someone from the parish did too. They were worried about whether I was safe, I guess. That’s hard to admit. I wasn’t exactly out living normal days, no.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced any strange thoughts or heard voices that others couldn't hear?\n\nPatient: I don’t know… strange thoughts, I’m not sure what counts as strange. I’ve had plenty of thoughts I don’t like, or ones that go around and around like a bad saw blade, but voices—like somebody talking when nobody’s there—I can’t say I’ve noticed that.\n\nSometimes in the shop you hear all kinds of things, compressors kicking on, a radio in the other room, someone hollering over a planer, and you think someone said your name when they didn’t. But that’s just noise, I think. Not like… a voice telling me things or anything like that.\n\nDoctor: Have you felt like you're moving or reacting slower than usual for most of the day, nearly every day?\n\nPatient: Yeah… people at work have noticed it, which is embarrassing. Some days I’m moving like my boots are full of wet cement, you know—taking forever just to pick up a board or get across the shop.\n\nAnd then other times I’m not exactly slow, I’m just… stuck pacing around, rubbing my hands, not getting anything useful done. Like I’ll walk past the same stack of lumber three times and still not make the cut. Reminds me of when the planer belt slips and the whole thing’s making noise but nothing’s feeding through right.\n\nSo yeah, I guess I have been slower—or off somehow—for a lot of the day.\n\nDoctor: Have you lost more than 5% of your body weight over the past six months?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure. I don’t weigh myself much—our scale’s probably buried under laundry somewhere, honestly. My belt maybe sits different some days, but then work pants stretch out, you know, especially when you’re kneeling and climbing around all day.\n\nFive percent… I don’t know what that would be for me without doing the math. So I can’t say I’ve noticed that kind of weight loss, no.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever heard voices or seen things that others cannot?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so. Like I said, I’ll hear a bang in the shop or think someone called out from the next room, but that’s usually just noise bouncing around. Seeing things… not really. Sometimes shadows move funny when you’re tired, especially around lumber racks and dust in the air, but I wouldn’t say I’m seeing things that aren’t there.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever seen or heard things that you couldn't explain and others couldn't see or hear?\n\nPatient: I mean… not really, no. I get startled by sounds sometimes—old houses do that, and shops are full of weird noises, pipes ticking, wood settling, wind pushing through a bad window frame. I’ve spent half my life figuring out whether a sound is a loose hinge or a board shifting.\n\nBut if you mean like something only I can hear or see and nobody else can… I don’t think so. Nothing I’d swear was there when it wasn’t.\n\nDoctor: Have you had any difficulty concentrating or making decisions?\n\nPatient: Yeah… decisions, concentration, that’s been rough. I’ll be looking at blueprints—stuff I’ve read my whole life, you know, framing plans, measurements, nothing new—and the lines just sort of blur together in my head. Not my eyes exactly, more like I can’t make it click.\n\nThen I’m standing there asking one of the younger guys to repeat something I should’ve understood the first time. That’s embarrassing. I’ve been doing this longer than some of them have been alive, and I’m still like, “Wait, run that by me again.”"
